**2026-10-16: T109 merged (PR #114): help pop-ups behind a question-mark icon.** The idea `docs/ideas/2026-10-13-help-popups.md`, built under ADR 0031: a new `androidApp/.../help/` package with `HelpText(key, title, placed, needs, show, text(units))`, `HelpRegistry` (gathering the screens' lists), `HelpUi`, `HelpIcon(key)`, `HelpHost` (the pop-up) and `HelpRoutes` (`GET /help`, `POST /help/open?key=`, `POST /help/close`). The icon is a question mark in a 24 dp ring at font scale 1.0, sized in sp so it grows with the font scale, inside a touch target of at least 48 dp (56 dp at 2.0; 51 dp on the map chips, which cap the scale at 1.3), spoken "Help: <title>" and not text in the accessibility tree, so the tap scripts never meet it; filled while its pop-up is open. The pop-up is a paper card (title, text, OK) below the icon when it fits, else above, with a caret on the icon; when the text fits on neither side (font scale 2.0, three of 24) it covers the icon rather than cut the text; a tap anywhere or Back closes it. Each screen declares its texts next to its controls (`MAP_OPTIONS_HELP`, `SETTINGS_HELP`, `DIRECT_SOURCES_HELP`, `AREAS_HELP`, `TRACK_BAR_HELP`, `LONG_PRESS_HELP`), so a removed control removes its text; 24 placements: every Map options row and the slope legend, Offline areas (the title and the corridor line), Settings (Sources, Mobile data, Limit per session, Units, Text size, Route snap, GPS fix stale after, Snooze, Sync status, Share, Contour warm-up), the unfolded track bar and the long-press card (Waypoint here, which the owner found hard to find). A distance in a text goes through `UnitFormat` in the units setting (the corridor's 500 m reads "500 m" or "0.3 mi"). `HelpRegistryTest` (9): every key a screen references has a text and every text a key, each text in the same file as its icon, one paragraph of 60 to 330 characters, no other product name, the units renderings; `user-text-check.py` 0 hits over 14,015 literals. Viewed at font scale 1.0 and 2.0: nine pop-ups, each `inside` and `textFits`; a uiautomator dump for all 24 icons at both scales found nothing over the icon and no label or other icon over its target (circle 24 dp in a 48 dp target at 1.0, 36 in 56 at 2.0). Found and fixed on the way: the Offline areas text cut at 2.0 (now the card covers the icon and the four longest texts were shortened); the icon's target met the chips' widened targets by up to 5 dp (6 dp more below a label row); `ScreenNamesTest` reads "›" as a screen path, so placements use ">". Release build by taps: `features` 12/12 (one run lost a step to the host's adb server restarting, clean when rerun), `permissions` 7/7, `matrix --font-scale 2.0` 6/6; device tour 10/10 before and after the rebase; `check` green. The verifier rebased over T112 (one docs conflict in `android-app.md`, both paragraphs kept), ran `check` and the user-text check, viewed seven pop-ups at both scales (every one `inside` and `textFits`; at 2.0 Offline areas and Sources cover their icon with the text whole), measured the targets with a uiautomator dump over Map options and five Settings pages (48 dp at 1.0, 56 at 2.0, nothing over them), repeated the release build by taps (12/12, 7/7, 6/6) and the device tour 10/10, and noted that `POST /help/open` leaves what it opened behind (the long-press card, the Offline areas sheet) while `/help/close` closes only the pop-up, that `map.slopeLegend` answers 409 until slope shading is on (as `needs` says), and that the mobile-data text says downloads happen only on Wi-Fi when the setting is off, which is what `directOnMobileData` does (the setting ADR 0023 keeps, default on). Follow-ups: `/help/close` should also dismiss what the open route opened; `MainActivity`'s `MapScreen` should call `HelpHost()` last (today it is called at the end of `AreasOverlay()`, the last overlay T109 owned, so the Share screen and QR scanner have no pop-up, and no icons either); help icons for the Storage screen's rows and the Data and storage subtitle (after T112); icons on the navigation bar's off-route banner and lost-fix line (`nav/**`); `firstrun/Screens.kt` could list the settings groups as sections so a placement may say "Settings › Map options"; in the track bar the icon's target meets the chips' widened targets by 2 dp, as the chips already do with each other; the look and the 24 texts for the owner (ADR 0031, the table in the PR).

**2026-10-16: v0.1.8-alpha published** (tag on 3b3f57a, versionCode 396; GitHub Releases: the universal APK (58.7 MB), the server and devtool zips, `NOTICES.md`, `SHA256SUMS.txt`, `version.json` and the world tour sheet, and no device tour sheet any more (T111); built by the release workflow; published 08:33 UTC). Phase-0 algorithms live in `shared` from the start, so the same code runs on Android in phase 1. Their speed and memory on the JVM are measured, then checked again on a real device.

## Agent-driven development
Agents can't tap a screen or watch a map scroll, so every feature also needs an interface an agent can drive.

### Principles
1. **Logic lives in `shared`, not the UI.** Anything in the shared module can be tested on the JVM in seconds, with no emulator. The UI layer stays thin.
2. **Every feature ships with its debug hooks and tests.** A feature isn't done until an agent can trigger it, observe the result, and check it automatically.
3. **Everything is deterministic and injectable.** Clock, GPS location, network state, file system, and random seeds are interfaces that tests can replace with fakes.
4. **Output an agent can read.** Structured JSON logs and state dumps, plus PNGs for anything visual (agents can view images).
5. **Debug tooling ships only in debug builds.** It's compiled out of release builds.

### Tooling layers
| Layer | What it is | Used for |
|---|---|---|
| JVM unit tests | `shared` module tests, run with Gradle | Geo math, DEM decoding, contour generation, sync logic, storage |
| Dev CLI (`devtool`) | JVM command-line app that calls into `shared` | Exists: `render`, `compare`, `areas`. Later: tile export, `sync-sim --peers 3`, PMTiles/GPX inspection |
| Golden-image tests | Rendered tiles and screens compared against approved PNGs | Map rendering, hillshade, contours, UI screens (Roborazzi/Paparazzi run Compose screenshots on the JVM) |
| Sync simulator | Many in-process peers over a fake network with latency, partitions, and reordering, driven by a seed | Proving offline/online convergence; any failure replays exactly from its seed |
| In-app debug server | Debug builds run a small HTTP/JSON server, reached via `adb forward` | Query state, invoke actions, inject GPS fixes and tracks, toggle offline mode, load fixtures, dump the DB, fetch logs, take screenshots, read map camera/layers |
| UI end-to-end | Maestro flows (YAML) or UI Automator, run against the emulator | Critical user journeys |
| Server debug endpoints | Same debug API on the Ktor server; local multi-peer setup via Docker Compose | Server-side state and sync inspection |

### Privacy in the repo
This is a public, open-source repo. **Nothing that reveals where the developer lives or travels is ever committed.**
- Personal test areas are defined in a gitignored local file (`*.local.*`, e.g. `devtool.local.json`). It can hold several named areas ("home", "trip") and be changed freely.
- Committed tests, fixtures, and golden images use only well-known public places. Initial defaults: **Mount Rainier, WA** (US, high-resolution USGS lidar) and **Mont Blanc / Chamonix** (non-US, Copernicus 30 m).
- Generated output, downloaded data, and caches go in gitignored directories (`out/`, `data/`, `cache/`).
- Test GPS tracks and waypoints are synthetic or come from public places. Real recorded tracks are never used as fixtures.
- The same rule applies to the app's debug tooling: logs and state dumps that may contain locations stay local.

### Command-line environment
- Gradle wrapper builds everything; no Android Studio required for agents (it's still useful for humans).
- Android SDK command-line tools + `adb` + headless emulator (`emulator -no-window`).
- Single entry points, e.g. `./gradlew check` runs everything that doesn't need a device; one script boots the emulator, installs the app, and opens the debug port.
- A `CLAUDE.md` at the repo root documents these commands and conventions for agents.

## Licensing
The project will be fully open source. Code and map content may need different licenses. Working choices: GPLv3 for apps, AGPLv3 for the server, LGPLv3 possible for `shared`; ODbL for OSM-derived vector tiles, CC BY-SA 4.0 for other content. These can be revisited later (see below).

### Hard rules
1. **Data compatibility.** Everything we produce must comply with the licenses of OSM (ODbL) and every elevation or other data source we use, including attribution.
2. **iOS compatibility.** Tiles, the sync protocol, and anything else other modules exchange must be usable by a future iPhone app. Tile formats and the wire protocol are open, documented specs, so any client can implement them regardless of the code license.
3. **Keep relicensing possible.** We can change the license later only as long as we remain the sole copyright holders. So:
   - Write our own code. Don't copy code from other projects into the repo.
   - Dependencies must be permissively licensed (MIT, BSD, Apache-2.0, EPL/EDL, etc.). **No GPL or AGPL dependencies anywhere.** Those would lock in our license choice. **LGPL is allowed only in build-side tooling** (`tilebuild`, later the server's tile pipeline) where the library stays a separate, replaceable jar; never in `shared`, the apps, or anything distributed to users. (Decided 2026-10-09 when Planetiler brought in GeoTools, LGPL-2.1: LGPL doesn't constrain the license of code that merely uses the library, so it doesn't threaten relicensing.)
   - No outside contributions until there's a contribution policy (CLA or copyright assignment).
   - An automated dependency-license check fails the build on any disallowed license. **Done (T7):** `./gradlew licenseCheck`, part of `check`; config in `gradle/licenses/`.
   - Versions already released under a license stay available under that license; relicensing applies going forward.
4. **Data source registry.** Every data source (DEM, OSM, overlays) is listed with its license, required attribution text, and allowed uses. Tile builds read attribution from this registry and write it into tile metadata.

### Code: GPL family
| Option | Effect | Fit |
|---|---|---|
| GPLv3-or-later | Anyone distributing modified apps must release their source | Default candidate for the apps |
| AGPLv3 | Also covers running modified code as a network service | Candidate for the server, so hosted forks must share changes |
| LGPLv3 | Others can use the library in closed apps; changes to the library itself must be shared | Candidate for the `shared` core if we want wide reuse |

- Use **v3**, not v2: Apache-2.0 dependencies (Planetiler, GraphHopper) are compatible with GPLv3 only.
- Expected dependencies are compatible with GPLv3: MapLibre (BSD-2), Planetiler (Apache-2.0), BRouter (MIT), GraphHopper (Apache-2.0), JTS (EPL-2.0/EDL dual license, use EDL).
- **iOS App Store vs GPL:** Apple's terms conflict with GPL distribution of code contributed by others. A sole copyright holder can grant Apple an exception or dual-license. Decide on a contribution policy (CLA or a GPL app-store exception) **before** taking outside contributions.
- F-Droid distribution works with any of these.

### Map content
| Source | License | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| OpenStreetMap | ODbL 1.0 | Vector tiles that contain OSM data are a derived database, so they **must stay ODbL** (share-alike). Rendered raster images are a "produced work" and can use any license, with OSM attribution. |
| USGS 3DEP / NED, SRTM | Public domain | No restrictions |
| Copernicus GLO-30 DEM | Free, attribution required | Fine for generated contours/hillshade |
| swisstopo swissALTI3D (and the CHGeo2004 geoid) | swisstopo OGD terms: free use incl. commercial, source required (CHGeo2004 grid: CC0) | Fine for generated contours/hillshade; credit swisstopo wherever Swiss data shows (registry ids `swissalti3d`, `chgeo2004`) |
| Kartverket national DTM (and the HREF2018B height reference grid) | CC BY 4.0 | Fine for generated contours/hillshade; credit Kartverket wherever Norwegian data shows (registry ids `kartverket-dtm`, `href2018b`) |
| BEV ALS DTM Austria (and BEV's ETRS89 geoid grid) | CC BY 4.0 | Fine for generated contours/hillshade; credit BEV wherever Austrian data shows (registry ids `bev-als-dtm`, `bev-geoid`) |
| NRCan HRDEM mosaic (and the CGG2013a geoid grid) | Open Government Licence - Canada 2.0 (SPDX `OGL-Canada-2.0`) | Free use including commercial; the attribution statement "Contains information licensed under the Open Government Licence - Canada" goes with Canadian data (registry ids `hrdem`, `cgg2013a`) |
| LINZ New Zealand LiDAR 1m DEM (and the NZGeoid2016 grid) | CC BY 4.0 | Fine for generated contours/hillshade; credit LINZ ("Sourced from LINZ. CC BY 4.0") wherever New Zealand data shows (registry ids `linz-dem`, `nzgeoid2016`) |
| Geoid grids from the PROJ CDN (GEOID18, GEOID12B, EGM2008, CHGeo2004, HREF2018B, BEV geoid, CGG2013a, NZGeoid2016) | Per agency: US public domain (NOAA, NGA), CC0 (swisstopo), CC BY 4.0 (Kartverket, BEV, LINZ), OGL-Canada 2.0 (NRCan) | Registry `licenseUrl` is the licence itself (T66: CC deeds, usa.gov's government-works page, the OGL-Canada page); the PROJ-data README that states it is named in each entry's notes |
| Natural Earth (admin-0 borders for the Norway clip, and tilebuild's low-zoom layers) | Public domain | No attribution required; registry id `natural-earth` |
| User-added third-party sources | Per provider | Many forbid caching or redistribution; user-added sources stay on the user's device and are never republished |

- **How the national 1 m sources are read (T59).** Norway: Kartverket publishes no range-readable COGs (hoydedata.no hands out zipped project blocks), but its WCS (`wcs.geonorge.no/skwms1/wcs.hoyde-dtm-nhm-25833`, coverage `nhm_dtm_topo_25833`, an ArcGIS image service) cuts any box at any resolution, so `NorwaySource` asks for fixed 256 px blocks on a UTM 33 grid at 1 to 16 m and caches each block forever; 0 is the service's fill (sea, and beyond a strip of coarser data that reaches 10 km or more into Sweden). Austria: BEV's ALS DTM tiles (`data.bev.gv.at/download/ALS/DTM/<edition>/`) are 7.7 GB Cloud-Optimized BigTIFFs on 50 km EPSG:3035 squares (not EPSG:3416 Austria Lambert), range-readable with overviews, so no whole-tile download is needed; heights are EVRF2000 Austria (EPSG:9274) from the 2022 edition on, GHA only in 2021. A whole-tile fallback was not needed for either.
- **Canada and New Zealand (T69).** Both are on the AWS open data registry and range-readable. Canada: NRCan's HRDEM mosaics (`canelevation-dem.s3.ca-central-1.amazonaws.com/hrdem-mosaic-{1,2}m/<i>_<j>-mosaic-{1,2}m-dtm.tif`, listed by the STAC API at `datacube.services.geo.ca/stac/api`) are one Cloud-Optimized BigTIFF per 500 km square of EPSG:3979 Canada Atlas Lambert (500000 px at 1 m, LZW, overviews to 512x, no-data -32767, sparse where no lidar was flown); the 1 m mosaic's tile index is about 15 MB per file, so it is read only for cells of 1 m or less, the 2 m mosaic (which also has the projects delivered at 2 m only) everywhere else. Heights are CGVD2013; PROJ's CGG2013a grid is relative to NAD83(CSRS), so the NAD83 -> WGS84 height shift is added as for the US hybrid geoids, and the grid is int32 with a GDAL scale (GeoTiff reads that now). Lidar coverage is patchy in the mountains: none at Mount Robson (Copernicus serves it), two blocks around Mount Garibaldi. 3DEP's 10 m base reaches well into southern British Columbia, so HRDEM goes over 3DEP in `best`. New Zealand: LINZ's national 1 m DEM mosaic (`nz-elevation.s3.ap-southeast-2.amazonaws.com/new-zealand/new-zealand/dem_1m/2193/<sheet>.tiff`) is one COG per NZTopo50 sheet (24 x 36 km of NZTM2000, 424 sheets), LERC-compressed (1 mm error, 10 cm in overviews), which our own `Lerc2` reader decodes; NZTM is UTM's transverse Mercator with a 2 degree longitude and 1100 km easting shift; heights are NZVD2016, converted with NZGeoid2016.
- **The phone reads the sources itself (T80).** With no Dystopo server set, the app reads the same public files the server does: the Copernicus GLO-30 COGs on AWS, USGS 3DEP 1/3" COGs and the 1 m lidar tiles through the TNM Access API, swisstopo's STAC API and COGs, Kartverket's WCS, BEV's download service, NRCan's and LINZ's COGs on AWS, the PROJ CDN geoid grids, and OpenFreeMap's public vector tiles (`tiles.openfreemap.org`, OpenMapTiles schema, ODbL data). The licences and required credits are the same rows as above; nothing is redistributed by us (each phone fetches for itself, a pack it builds stays on that phone), the map line credits what the view shows and the About screen lists every source the phone can read with its licence. Requests carry a `dystopo-android` user agent, at most 16 at once, and a phone downloads at most 300 MB per session (setting), so the public services see one person's map use, not a mirror.
- The likely result: **ODbL for vector tiles** (forced by OSM), and **CC BY-SA 4.0** (the share-alike, GPL-like content license) or CC BY 4.0 for raster tiles and non-OSM layers.
- Each tile set records its sources and attribution in its metadata, and the app shows attribution for every visible layer.

## Design goals (set 2026-10-10): one data path online and offline, precaching, long routes, sharing on a trip

The owner's longer-term goals, written down so that near-term work stays friendly to them. None of this is scheduled as a single task; the principles bind every task that touches tiles, caches, packs, routing or sharing, and the gaps are on the board (T73 to T78).

**Goals.** (1) Move between online and offline without the user noticing: whatever was fetched or built is kept, and what is likely to be needed is fetched ahead. (2) Keep what we build (contours, shade, cliffs, graphs) so nothing is rebuilt on every zoom; these caches are keyed by every setting that affects them and can be thrown away at any time. (3) On the phone, the disk the caches may use is a setting. (4) Let users say where they are going and download everything for that area or route ahead of time (what CalTopo and Backcountry Navigator call offline maps). (5) Route searches longer than one pack: rim to rim across the Grand Canyon on foot, a bike ride between two Colorado towns 40 km apart, a drive from New York to Los Angeles. (6) On a trip, one phone that holds the sources (packs, and maybe generated tiles) serves the others over the LAN or discovered Bluetooth, so a group is effectively offline together; whether to share generated tiles or regenerate locally is a question of relative cost, not principle.

**What already fits (as built through wave 11).** The phone's loopback server reads every installed pack tile by tile (`PackSet.demCandidates`/`osmCandidates`, precedence by profile, primary flag, zoom and area) and falls back to the online server only for tiles no pack covers; fetched DEM, vector and glyph tiles go into an LRU disk cache (`TileCache`, `onlineCacheMb`, default 256 MB), and contours over fetched DEM are generated on the phone and cached too. Derived caches are already keyed by what matters: contours by version, unit, tile size, interval and a DEM identity (CRC of size, mtime and PMTiles header); shade by hillshade mode, intensity, slope, night and DEM identity; cliffs by vector and DEM identity; one LRU with a user-set cap (`contourCacheMb`, default 256 MB) and garbage collection of directories whose pack no longer exists. "Download this region" builds a pack for the current view box on the server (`POST /build`, Overpass under the cap, else Geofabrik extracts on disk or downloaded on demand) and installs it with resumable, hash-verified downloads. Routes are stored as geometry (`path` field in the sync log), so navigation never depends on the router that produced them. For sharing, the phone already runs an HTTP tile server on loopback, a LAN share listener with NSD discovery and QR pairing (T54, encrypted since T72), and the pack catalog format with hashes and sidecars that the server uses; the phone could publish the same catalog.

**Principles from here on.**
1. *Everything on the phone is a cache in front of a source, and a pack is a pre-filled, pinned, immutable cache segment.* New code reads through one resolver (pack union, then disk cache, then a source) and never special-cases "the active pack". The primary pack survives only as a tie-breaker and as the camera's home; anything still keyed to it (contour warm-up, `RoutePlanner.ensure`) is a defect to remove, not a pattern to copy. A "source" is the server today; a peer phone on the LAN is another source behind the same interface (goal 6), which is why the online fetcher must not assume a single fixed server URL.
2. *Every cache key names its inputs and its version.* Settings vector plus source identity plus format version, so a change anywhere invalidates exactly what it must. Online tiles must carry the source's content version (today the online cache has no server version and no TTL, so a server DEM change is invisible until eviction); style assets must be versioned (today glyphs and sprites are never refetched if a file exists). Content hashes, not URLs, identify pack files, so the same pack from the server or from a peer is the same pack.
3. *Derived caches are never required for correctness and are always bounded.* Phone caches are bounded today; the server's `cache/live` and OpenFreeMap caches are not, and server contours and shade are not cached at all. Every store gets a cap and LRU, and one phone settings screen shows and sets the budgets (tiles, derived, packs) with what each is using. No Wi-Fi gates (owner, 2026-10-11): everything except LAN discovery works over mobile networks; data use is steered by visible limits the user can raise, never by a network-type check that silently fetches nothing. Generated tiles may be shared between phones only when the full derived key matches (same settings vector, same source identity); otherwise the receiving phone regenerates. On today's numbers (contour tile about 80 ms warm on the phone, a few KB each) regeneration usually wins over transfer; share sources first, derived tiles only where measured to help. T78 measured it (Sync › Peer sources on a trip): transfer beat regeneration only for tiles the peer already held, by at most 23 ms a tile, so derived tiles are not shared.
4. *An area of interest is a first-class object.* A box or a route corridor with a zoom range becomes a manifest (DEM, vector, glyphs, sprites, graph, search index, derived tiles optional), with progress, verification and a pinned flag that keeps it out of eviction until the user unpins it. "Download this region" is the box case of this; route corridors ("I am walking this") and "ahead of me along the route" prefetch are the next two cases. The server side is the existing `/build` plus per-area derived products. A manifest is also what one phone offers another: "I have these areas" is the catalog, and fetching an area from a peer is the same download code with a different base URL.
5. *Routing is tiled, not per pack.* The graph becomes graph tiles at a fixed zoom (built per pack as today but stored and loaded per tile, so a search spans packs and loads only what it touches), behind a `Router` interface with the current A* as one implementation. Long distance needs a hierarchy (contraction hierarchies or customizable route planning over the road classes) or a remote router behind the same interface, with the result saved as geometry plus a corridor download, so the drive from New York to Los Angeles is planned online once and navigated offline. The rim-to-rim walk and the 40 km ride fit one pack or two adjacent ones and only need the tiled graph and cross-pack stitching.
6. *Transports are pluggable and discovery is separate from transfer.* The sync layer already has file, HTTP and LAN transports with NSD discovery; pack and tile sharing uses the same shape: discover a peer (NSD now, Bluetooth LE advertising later, where the Bluetooth link carries only the discovery and pairing and the bulk transfer runs over Wi-Fi Direct or the LAN, because Bluetooth throughput is far too low for packs), verify what it offers by content hash, fetch over HTTP from the peer's loopback server made reachable on the LAN interface with the same per-session token and encryption as the share link. As built (T78): the share listener itself publishes the catalog, area manifests, archive pieces and pack tiles inside the sealed link (no second listener, no second secret), a peer is a `peer://` source of the online fetcher tried before the network, and packs come through the same resumable, hash-checked downloader; the Bluetooth LE design (discovery and pairing only) is written down there, not built.

**Gaps, on the board.** T74 cache coherence and budgets (versioned online tiles and assets, bounded server caches, cached online shade, the storage settings screen); T75 areas of interest (manifests, corridors, pinning, prefetch along a route); T76 tiled routing graph and cross-pack routes behind a `Router` interface; T77 long-distance routing (hierarchy or remote router, corridor download); T78 peer sources on a trip (phone publishes its catalog on the LAN, other phones add it as a source, measured decision on sharing derived tiles); T73 drive mode is the third travel mode and a prerequisite for the drive case.

**Areas of interest as built (T75, proposal for the ADR T84 will write).** An area is a box or a route corridor (a line and a buffer in metres) with a zoom range; its id is a hash of the geometry and zooms, not the name, so the same area asked twice or by two phones is one area. It expands to a versioned manifest (`AreaManifest`, field by field in docs/SERVER.md › Areas of interest) whose items are `pack` archives with SHA-256, `tiles` lists per route and zoom (rows `[y, x0, x1]`) with estimated sizes, bundled style assets, and products the phone makes itself (contours, trail graph, search index). A box is a region pack built through `/build` (the server answers `POST /area` with 202 while it builds, then the archives with hashes, so the phone's download is verified like a catalog download); a corridor is the DEM and vector tiles within the buffer (DEM from z6, because contour tiles sample coarser zooms than they show, and one tile of margin), fetched through the online fetcher into the tile cache and **pinned** there: the cache's eviction asks a spatial predicate (`AreaPins`) whether a key's z/x/y lies in a pinned area's cover, so DEM, vector and contour tiles of any source and any settings stay with one rule and no key lists. During navigation the next 3 km of the line (1 km segments, 300 m buffer, z12 to z15) are fetched ahead while online and pinned until navigation stops. Rejected: (a) a corridor as a pack of only its tiles, because the resolver ranks packs by their bounding box, so such a pack would shadow online tiles beside the corridor inside its box (blank vector tiles, coarse synthesized terrain even online) until the resolver checks per-tile presence; (b) a corridor as the pack of its bounding box, right for a short loop and wrong for a long drive, whose box is half a continent; (c) explicit key lists per area, which depend on the source URL and on every derived setting and grow with every contour tile; (d) per-tile sizes and hashes in the manifest, too large for long corridors and unknown to a server that renders tiles on demand (tiles are checked by type instead, packs by SHA-256); (e) a separate pinned store outside the cap, cleaner accounting but a second resolver lookup or a cache rewrite, left to T74, which owns the budgets: today pinned tiles count against `onlineCacheMb` and are skipped by eviction, so a cap below the pinned areas leaves the cache over its cap, visible in `GET /areas`. Packs stay until removed (unpinning a pack would mean moving its tiles into the LRU, which costs more than it saves).

**Pinned tiles and content versions (T95, 2026-10-14; decision record: ADR 0030, which supersedes ADR 0027 in part).** The critic's blocking objection (`design/proposals/0027-critic-review.md`): a source's content version change (any `/build` changes the server's `dem` version, a new OpenFreeMap planet its `osm` version) deleted every tile of the old version, pinned corridors included, and lookups used the current version anyway. The rule now: a pinned area records, per source, the content versions (`dem`, `osm` as `GET /version` names them; "" for a source without versions) its tiles were fetched at (`Areas.Record.versions`; the manifest carries the source's as `versions`), and its tiles stay keyed by them until the area is refreshed. When a source's version changes, the online fetcher keeps the `dem` and `osm` tiles a pin holds at the old version (`AreaPins.holds`: the pin's cover, as for eviction, at the version it recorded) and drops the rest as before; contour and shade tiles made on the phone from the old DEM still go, and are made again from the kept DEM. A lookup that misses the current version tries the versions the pins hold before the network, online as well as offline, so an area shows one version throughout and costs no data until it is refreshed. A refresh fetches the current version only (`fresh`, never a pinned older one): the area's Update (its card says "Older than the source" when a recorded version differs from what the source serves, and the Storage screen's pinned row names it), or the prefetch ahead on the route while navigating online, segment by segment. A download that completes whole with no version change on the way records the versions it now holds; then the contour and shade tiles made in its cover at the current version (perhaps from the older DEM a pin served) and the old version's tiles that no pin holds are deleted (`OnlineFetcher.dropUnheld`, also run after Unpin and Remove). Transient pins of the prefetch record their versions the same way. Areas recorded before T95 take the old version at the first change. Rejected: re-fetching a stale area in the background as soon as the source moves on (data the user did not ask for, and an area left half old and half new when the network drops; Update and the prefetch are visible and bounded); trying every older version of a missing tile (an index of the cache by z/x/y, where the pins already name the few versions that matter); keeping old-version contour and shade tiles under the pins (the map looks them up at the current version only, so they would be dead weight). The trip's Offline button, the critic's serious objection: it first works out the corridor's manifest and shows its size ("This route offline: about 29 MB (240 tiles)." with Download and Cancel), or, when the pinned budget (`pinnedCacheMb`, T91) would be passed, the size and the reason with Close; nothing is recorded until Download, and the corridor uses T91's per-mode zooms (z8 to z13 for a drive, z8 to z15 otherwise). As a straight line (a lower bound) with the trip card's 550 m, a drive of about 630 km from Rainier to Glacier/Waterton is about 310 MB at z8 to z13 against 1,223 MB at z8 to z15, which the default 2 GB budget refuses once 1.5 GB is pinned (`OfflineOfferTest`; the critic estimated 930 MB for a 570 km drive at z8 to z15). Not done: pinned bytes per source in `GET /areas` (a source switch leaves the old source's pinned tiles unreachable until Unpin; the critic's hidden coupling), and an area recorded at one server is not called older when the phone now uses another. T106 did both: `GET /areas` lists the pinned bytes per source key (`pinnedBySource`, with `inUse`), an area fetched from a source the phone no longer reads is older in every name it holds ("Downloaded from another source", Update), and its whole Update deletes the old source's tiles in its cover that no other area fetched from there holds, since pins are spatial and would otherwise keep them, unreachable, in the pinned budget; an unpinned area stays unpinned until the budget lets its Update go on.

### Caches (T112, 2026-10-16)

Principle 3 above and ADR 0025 bound every cache; ADR 0034 amends it for the phone: one total budget (`cacheBudgetMb`; presets 1 GB by default, 8 GB, 128 GB, a custom size or a share of the free space picked once) over three kinds, online tiles, contours and shading (wherever the phone keeps them, packs' and online ones alike) and the phone's source downloads, with floors of 25, 15 and 5% of the total; when the total is passed, contours and shading go first, then the source kind furthest above its floor, least recently used first, never a tile in view or within 300 m of the next 3 km of a navigated route, never a pinned one (pinned areas and packs are outside, ADR 0030). The per-cache caps stay as ceilings. Measured with `devtool cache-replay` over the standalone server's log of 23 viewing sessions (10,540 tile answers; scripted tours, phone-style tours and six seeded random walks over public places): against one LRU with no floors the floors keep contours (33% of contour requests hit on the random walks at 32 MB against 1.6%, all a cache of everything would get) for at most 3 points of online hits; a size-weighted frequency score (GDSF) instead of LRU within a kind gains 4 to 7 points of request hits and 0.6 to 3.5 points of byte hits on the random walks, more on the scripted tours replayed in the same order (LRU's worst case, a loop a little larger than the cache), and loses its byte gain when its counts do not survive an app restart (33.2% against LRU's 34.5% of bytes at 64 MB over all 23 sessions). So LRU stays within a kind, and the cache stays plain files whose times carry the order across restarts: no index database until a request log from a real phone (wave 16) shows the frequency gain on real use. The full table is in ADR 0034.

### The phone reads the sources (T80, measured 2026-10-10)

With no server the app renders DEM tiles from the shared `best` source (`androidApp/.../online/DirectSources.kt`, a second target behind `OnlineFetcher`) and fetches OpenFreeMap tiles itself; a configured server takes precedence. Measured on an x86_64 API 35 emulator on the development machine (host to S3 about 0.9 MB/s per connection; the emulator about 0.2 MB/s per request with 16 in flight), cold source cache, one z12 view (1080 x 2400 screen) after "Show the map around me":

| View | Source bytes | DEM tiles rendered | Avg / max per tile | Settled with contours (release, taps) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Rainier, 3DEP 10 m (default: DEM rendered to z12, z13 synthesized) | 66 to 83 MB | 41 to 49 (z8 4, z9 1, z10 12, z11 12, z12 12 to 20) | 6 to 8 s / 12 to 19 s | 67 to 69 s |
| Rainier with 3DEP 1 m lidar (`directMaxZoom` 15: z13 rendered) | 93 MB | 64 | 7.5 s / 18 s | not run by taps |
| Mont Blanc, Copernicus + swissALTI3D | 96 to 103 MB | 41 to 45 | 7 s / 16 s | 61 to 68 s |
| Rainier again, source cache warm, rendered tiles dropped | 0.2 MB (OpenFreeMap 1.5 MB) | 48 | 4.4 s / 6.4 s (CPU: decoding, geoid offsets, compositing) | |
| Downloaded box (16 km, DEM z8 to 12, map z8 to 14) right after the view | mostly from the online cache | | | 7 to 11 s to install; offline view settled in 13 to 20 s |
| T85 before, same emulator 2026-10-12 (debug build, cache cleared): Rainier | 81.4 MB, 151 requests | 49 (z8 4, z9 1, z10 12, z11 12, z12 20) | 6.5 s / 15 s | release by taps: first contours 62 s, settled 67 s (coarse first-view lines, 2% of pixels) |
| T85 before: Mont Blanc | 101.4 MB, 130 requests | 48 (z8 4, z9 1, z10 12, z11 15, z12 16) | 8.2 s / 15 s | first contours 58 s, settled 64 s (coarse, 2%) |
| T85 after: Rainier (contours read the hillshade's zoom, no halo ring, coarser overview below z12) | 21.6 to 28.0 MB, 55 to 68 requests (three runs, 26 to 34% of before) | 18 to 19 (z8 1, z9 4, z11 1 to 2, z12 12) | 6.4 s / 12 s | first contours 28 s, settled 48 s (full z12 contours, 10%) |
| T85 after: Mont Blanc | 38.8 to 46.4 MB, 82 to 86 requests (38 to 46%) | 20 (z9 4, z11 4, z12 12) | 7 s / 15 s | first contours 31 s, settled 38 s (full, 10%) |
| T85 after, z10 view of Rainier (`/camera`): before the coarser overview / with it | 55.6 MB / 28.4 to 29.2 MB | 35 (z11 24) | 6.8 s / 5.0 s | |
| T88 before: main (T85's code) on 2026-10-14, release by taps, Rainier | as T85 after | as T85 after | | first contours 5.6 s (the coarse lines only), 20.0, 20.6, 23.7 s; full 21.5 to 24.7 s; settled 24.7 to 29.9 s (four runs, two of them with four other emulators on the host) |
| T88 before: Mont Blanc | as T85 after | as T85 after | | first contours 8.4, 11.8, 15.3, 16.9 s; full 8.4 to 19.8 s |
| T88 after: Rainier (one emulator on the host, link 1.1 to 1.5 MB/s) | 17.5 MB: 12.7 MB in 29 source reads, 4.1 MB OpenFreeMap; 47 requests | 16 (z9 4, z12 12) | z12 2.5 to 3.8 s / 4.7 to 5.5 s (release, logcat trace) | first contours 5.6, 7.1, 7.6 s; full 10.4, 11.9, 13.9 s; settled 13.4, 16.1, 16.1 s |
| T88 after: Mont Blanc (link 1.4 to 2.2 MB/s) | 19.4 MB: 17.5 MB in 42 reads, 0.5 MB OpenFreeMap; 61 requests | 16 (z9 4, z12 12) | z12 2.7 to 4.5 s / 3.7 to 7.3 s | first contours 4.1, 4.7, 6.7 s; full 6.3 to 12.3 s; settled 12.0 to 15.6 s |
| T88 builds with four other emulators on the host (link 0.8 to 1.6 MB/s): Rainier | as above | as above | z12 2.8 to 6.5 s / 5.9 to 11.3 s | first contours 4.5 to 22.5 s, within 15 s in 10 of 17 runs; main in the same conditions 20.0 and 23.7 s |

The verifier measured the same cold Rainier z12 view at 77.5 MB in 150 requests, and split a 4 by 5 tile area by DEM zoom: z12 25.1 MB, z11 10.0 MB, z10 7.0 MB, z9 5.5 MB, so most of a view is the z8 to z11 ancestors read for contour sampling, not the hillshade's own tiles. OpenFreeMap tiles were about 4 MB of each view (low-zoom planet tiles are 0.8 MB each). Because a fresh view is 75 to 100 MB, direct reading is Wi-Fi only by default (`directOnMobileData`), and the per-session limit is a visible setting (`directSessionMb`, 300 MB) with a message on the map when it is reached. Most of the source bytes are whole COG blocks: a 3DEP 1/3" block is about 0.8 MB, a Copernicus block about 2.5 MB, and a z12 view touches dozens once the contour tiles' coarser sample zooms (z10, z11) are counted. The 15 s goal of the task was not reached on this emulator: the time is network-bound (the summed request time was 300 to 450 s over 16 connections), not CPU-bound; a phone on fast Wi-Fi should be several times faster, and with a warm source cache a render still takes about 4 s of emulator CPU, so a fresh view of a known place costs about a third of the cold time; tiles already rendered come from the online cache at once. What was done to cap it: Copernicus is skipped where a regional layer covers the whole window (same output; half the bytes of a US view), open COGs and their decoded blocks are pooled (96 files, 96 MB at most, a quarter of the heap), DEM tiles are rendered to z12 and synthesized deeper (`directMaxZoom`, 8 to 15), six renders and 16 source requests at once, map tiles never wait behind renders, a tile not ready in 8 s answers 503 and the map reloads when rendering is done (MapLibre does not retry a timed-out tile), 300 MB per session (`directSessionMb`), and the source cache (`cache/sources/`) is trimmed to 512 MB. Next steps that would cut bytes further, first one first: contour plans that sample the hillshade's own zoom when the phone renders terrain (the ancestors above are most of the bytes), then a coarser overview level for interactive phone views. T85 (2026-10-12) did both and found a third: the 4-cell halo of every contour tile read a whole ring of neighbouring DEM tiles, 14 of a z12 view's 20 renders. With direct sources a contour tile now reads the DEM at the hillshade's zoom (`ContourTileOptions.sampleAtDemZoom`; the grid is box-averaged down to the plan's, so levels and line counts match the pack: 11 Rainier tiles at z10 to z12, the same levels in every tile, 1228 lines against 1245, worst tile 9.9% fewer at z10), takes tiles the hillshade made or is making, renders a missing one only when it is in view or under a tile at the view's zoom (or MapLibre's fallback four zooms up), and leaves the out-of-view halo NaN (lines stop half a cell short of a seam nobody sees). Tiles MapLibre asks for while the camera is still moving are answered 503 once the view has settled elsewhere, and their queued renders are given up. DEM tiles below z12 read the overview for twice their cell size (it paid: a z10 view halves, a z12 view loses its z9 fallback bytes; hillshade at z10 shows no visible loss). Two older faults surfaced: MapLibre keeps a tile it got a 503 for across a reload of an identical style, so the T80 reload after rendering never reloaded anything (the reload count is now in the tile URLs; the first views before T85 showed only coarse fallback contours), and a contour 503 had to wait under MapLibre's 10 s timeout (6 s now). The 15 s target is still missed on this emulator: first contours 28 to 31 s, the time going to six concurrent 6 to 15 s renders.

T88 (2026-10-14) found where the time went and met the 15 s target on this emulator, 3 of 3 at Rainier and at Mont Blanc on the final build (rows above). `GET /firstrun/direct` now reports per-stage sums and a timeline (docs/reference/android-online.md; release builds write it to logcat), and a cold Rainier z12 view on the debug build breaks down as follows (summed over threads, so beyond the elapsed time):

| Stage | Before (main with the stage timers, 2026-10-13) | After (T88, 2026-10-14) |
|---|---|---|
| DEM renders | 17, average 8.4 s, max 12.5 s | 16, average 4.6 s, max 6.9 s |
| Sampling the sources into the window | 79.6 s (5.7 M synchronized block lookups by string key, a cell name built per pixel) | 3.2 s |
| Cold source reads | 80.9 s over 40 reads | 55.0 s over 29 reads (12.7 MB) |
| Block decoding | 1.9 s | 1.0 s |
| Geoid offsets | 5.6 s | 3.5 s (their blocks read with the cells') |
| Waiting for a render permit | 49.7 s | 9.3 s, 9.0 s of it the coarse-first hold |
| Contour tiles waiting for their DEM | 52.8 s | 50.3 s |
| Longest queue before a request ran (server pools) | tile pool 7.9 s, generation pool 9.0 s (T88's first commits, before the wait pool) | tile pool 29 ms, wait pool 56 ms |

What changed, by measured cause: sampling reads the four bilinear neighbours from one block through a per-thread memo of the last block (no lock per pixel) and names 3DEP cells per row and column; renders that start together share one download and one decode of a block; the geoid grids are read with the cells' blocks; source connections are kept alive and a range read with no byte for 4 s is retried (one read in five sat for 5 to 10 s while the same size took 0.4 s on another connection). The loopback server's eight tile workers and three generation workers were all held by DEM and contour requests waiting on renders, so OpenFreeMap tiles and the other contour tiles queued for up to 9 s and some passed MapLibre's 10 s read timeout, after which it never asks again: such requests now take a growing wait pool, direct DEM requests have their own threads, and the fetcher lets 48 requests through. Renders: 10 at once; a deeper tile renders its ancestor and in-view neighbours together; coarse tiles (below z12, the fallback MapLibre draws first) get a 1.5 s head start and their block reads go first in the source pool's queue (no hold at all gave Rainier first contours of 13.3 to 14.8 s in three runs, the coarse lines arriving no earlier than the fine ones; a 3 s hold gave 6.0 to 15.3 s and started the view's own tiles late on a fast link). A 503 costs a style reload, which blanks the map for 2 to 5 s on the emulator (MapLibre did not ask for a 503 tile again by itself in any trace), so the waits are 9 s for a DEM tile and 7 s for a contour tile's DEM (from 8 and 6), the reload comes once the renders the 503s waited on are done rather than every render, and 503s answered to the old style's requests bring no second reload. The floor on this emulator is the link: the view needs 12.7 MB of source blocks and 4.1 MB of OpenFreeMap tiles, which the emulator read at 1.1 to 2.2 MB/s with one emulator on the host and 0.8 to 1.6 MB/s with four others running (the host reads eight 0.8 MB 3DEP blocks in 1.2 s, 5 MB/s); at 1 MB/s the bytes alone take 17 s, and a view whose renders end after their wait adds one reload. Not changed: the z12 hillshade still reads 3DEP one overview down (0.8 MB blocks, nine for this view); a coarser level there would cut the bytes by about four and change what the hillshade shows.

Measured with `tools/android-firstrun.py --direct` (fresh install, taps) through a wrapper that keeps its flow and replaces its frame clock: the script decodes each screenshot PNG in Python (1 to 4 s a frame without Pillow), so its first-contours time is late by up to that; the wrapper grabs raw frames (`screencap` without `-p`, 0.7 to 1.5 s a frame) and also reports full contours, the first frame with half the settled contour share, since MapLibre's coarse fallback lines alone already count as first contours. Both clocks start about 0.8 s after the tap (the script's sleep after `input tap`).

The device tour's cold z8 view on a fresh emulator (T87's idle miss, no server, Rainier packs) missed idle on main too (74 s). What the map waited for: its contour tiles and MapLibre's z7 fallback ones overlap the pack, so they were made the pre-T85 way, whole plan windows with halo rings: 24 coarse z8 DEM renders (34 MB) of 40 to 75 s each in the debug build, holding every render permit while the view's own z9 hillshade tiles waited 70 s for one, then a reload after the last. Since T88 such tiles read the hillshade's zoom like the other direct tiles and take the pack's tile wherever the pack answers one: 9 renders, 9.7 MB, the view complete after 49 s (heap 64 MB at the tour's end, from 122 MB). It still misses the tour's 30 s on a just-booted emulator, where the debug build decodes a COG block in about 4 s (0.1 s once the JIT has warmed up) while the system's first-boot work takes two of the four cores; the next tour on the same emulator was 10/10 with the z8 view in 10 s.

## Units (decided 2026-10-10)
Feet versus metres is a **user preference, not a geographic rule**. Region only seeds the default: the browser demo picks feet inside the US and metres elsewhere the first time the page is opened and then keeps whatever the user chose (persisted); the phone seeds its default once from the home area if known (first pack opened, or first GPS fix) and never changes it on its own. Travelling keeps your units. Everything that shows an elevation or distance (contour labels and intervals, peak elevations, track stats, profiles, search results) follows the one setting. T34 shipped the by-region default; T33 (phone) and the viewer follow-up make it a persisted preference. Distances follow one rule, `UnitFormat.distance` (T101): metric reads in metres below 1 km, then in kilometres with two decimals below 10, one below 100 and none above; imperial in feet below a tenth of a mile (528 ft), then in miles with one decimal below 100 and none above; elevations and contour labels stay in feet or metres at any size, and the preference keeps its two values. The phone's scale bar reads in the unit that rule gives its length, at 1, 2 or 5 times a power of ten fitted to 100 dp.

## Tech stack
| Area | Choice | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Language | Kotlin (Java libraries where useful, JVM targets only) | Decided |
| Code sharing | Kotlin Multiplatform | Decided; in use (JVM target now, Android and iOS targets added later) |
| Android UI | Jetpack Compose (Compose Multiplatform later?) | In use (T10) |
| Map renderer | MapLibre Native 11 (Android); MapLibre GL JS in the browser demo | In use (T10) |
| Local DB | SQLDelight (SQLite) | In use (T31) |
| Sync | Own op log + HLC, per-field LWW, file and HTTP transports | Built (T44, hardened T51); see [Sync](#sync-as-built-t44-t51) |
| Server | Ktor (Kotlin, JVM) | Proposed |
| Tile generation | Elevation: own code in `shared` (working). OSM: Planetiler on server/desktop | Elevation done on desktop; Planetiler is task T6 |
| Routing | BRouter or GraphHopper | Future |
| Build | Gradle 8.14 (Kotlin DSL), JDK 21, Kotlin 2.2 | Decided |
| Package ID | `io.github.jasondorjeshort.dystopo` | Decided (GitHub namespace) |
| Min SDK | 26 | Decided (T10) |

## Open questions
1. Which "simple features" is BackCountry Navigator missing? What does each reference app do that must be kept?
2. What does "local map generation" mean: on the phone itself, or on infrastructure you control (your own server/desktop) instead of third-party tile providers?
3. ~~What does "distributed" mean?~~ Decided: ideally peer-to-peer with no central server (BitTorrent-like); a server is an optional always-on peer. Not built early. See [Peer-to-peer](#peer-to-peer-long-term-goal).
4. ~~Region~~ Decided: global, using the best available data for each region.
5. ~~Open source?~~ Decided: fully open source, with working license choices that can be revisited. See [Licensing](#licensing).
6. ~~App name~~ Decided: **Dystopo** (dystopia + topographic); tagline TBD. Package ID: `io.github.jasondorjeshort.dystopo`.
7. Bare-earth elevation outside the US: Copernicus includes tree canopy. Which national lidar sources to add first?
